%%TITLE: The Enneagram Is a Theorem
%%SUBTITLE: The complete mathematics of the nine-point figure, drawn in full and machine-verified throughout — the line theorem, the two orders, the gallery, the sixty forms and their twelve classes, the forms as diagrams, and the grading that reaches the color of matter — and the question whether the figure can carry a unitary expression of physics: its criteria, its correspondences, its completeness and a living tradition.
%%META: Christian Horstmann · thefirsthorstmann@gmail.com · July 2026 · version 4, October 2026, the definitive edition · manuscript for the public record
%%ABSTRACT: The enneagram is a figure of nine points on a circle, with a six-point path 1→4→2→8→5→7 and a triangle 3–6–9. It has circulated for a century through esoteric literature, its construction from the period of 1/7 expounded obliquely, never derived. Part I treats the figure as a combinatorial object of modular arithmetic. The line theorem characterizes unicursality: the figure of a prime p in base b consists of exactly (p−1)/ord_p(b) closed strokes, one exactly when b is a primitive root modulo p. Further chapters treat Midy's reflection, the gallery of multiplication, the ladder, the rings, the spectrum, the cyclotomic tower and the register. The sixty closed forms fall into twelve classes under the figure's twelve symmetries and are the orderings of a one-loop diagram with six legs, the alternating forms the color-flow basis of three quarks and three antiquarks. Z/9 admits exactly one proper nontrivial quotient, whose kernel is the triangle, so a charge structure that coarsens the figure's residues into more than one class has threefold multiplicity. Identified with color charge, an identification stated as such, the grading forbids a fourth color and an observable free quark. Part II asks whether the figure can carry a unitary expression of a physical theory: it states the criteria, charts what is established and what is open, proves the theory of the figure complete, states a hypothesis on the atomic mass unit, and names a living ninefold tradition where the question can be put. Every exact statement re-derives in a public suite at github.com/thefirsthorstmann/g-theory-verify.
%%

# PART I · THE FIGURE

## 1 · Introduction

The nine-point figure is familiar from a literature in which it is
presented rather than derived. Gurdjieff introduced it in the 1910s as a
diagram of process, and the presentations that follow from Ouspensky
onward reproduce its construction — nine points, a six-point path, a
triangle — without proving what fixes any of the three. The figure is
drawn, and its properties are asserted in a manner anyone familiar with
this enigmatic Greek-Armenian teacher will recognize: they are explained at
length in the teaching's own terms, obliquely and by evident intention,
and nowhere derived.

The mathematics the figure rests on is older and is not in dispute. The
repeating expansion of 1/7 has been studied since the eighteenth
century. Midy's theorem on the complementary halves of a prime
reciprocal of even period dates from 1836. The multiplicative structure
of the residues modulo nine, the epicycloid envelopes of multiplication
diagrams, the continued-fraction approximation of log ratios, and the
transpositional stabilizers of pitch-class sets are each standard
results in their own fields. What has not been assembled is the account
of the figure that these results jointly give.

This volume assembles it. The claim throughout is mathematical: that the
figure's construction is determined by the arithmetic it draws, that its
parts are theorems rather than conventions, and that the objects it
connects are connected for reasons that can be stated and checked. What
the figure means is not the subject. Two physical identifications are
made, the pair layer with the primordial fluctuations in §9 and the grading with color charge in
§18, each labeled there as an identification.† The other
physical readings are made in the companion records and are named at the
close. Part II, from §22, asks whether the figure can carry a unitary expression of a physical theory, and states the standing of each of its parts.

<div class="vnote">† Version 3 said that no physical reading is offered
in this volume. Sections 9 and 18 offer one each, labeled as
identifications.</div>

**What would refute this volume.** Every mathematical statement in it is
exact, so a single arithmetic error refutes the statement carrying it
and any statement downstream. The identifications of §9 and §18 and the readings of §18
are labeled as such and are tested by the measurements listed there.
Section 20 names the public suite in which each is checked, and the
reader with Python can re-derive the volume's entire exact content. Two
weaker claims are marked where they occur. The gallery's large-modulus
limit is stated as a limit and not as an identity, and the numerical
coincidences recorded at the end of §17 are recorded precisely so that
they are not mistaken later for results.

A condition that is met is recorded as a refutation and is not
reinterpreted. The program then uses the miss to find why the
construction failed, and a revised statement enters as a new one under
its own date.†

<div class="vnote">† Added in version 4.</div>

## 2 · The figure: nine points, the hexad and the triad, and the mirror

**Definition: the figure is nine points on a circle at equal spacing of 40°, labeled 1 through 9 with 9 at the top.** Two subsets partition the nine residues,
9 standing for 0: the *hexad* {1, 2, 4, 5, 7, 8} and the *triad* {3, 6,
9}.

**The traditional drawing joins the hexad in the order 1 → 4 → 2 → 8 → 5 → 7 → 1 and the triad as a triangle.** Both are
theorems, not conventions, as §4 states.

**Definition: the mirror is the reflection d ↔ 9 − d.** It fixes 9 (as 0) and exchanges the
hexad in pairs {1,8}, {2,7}, {4,5}; the triad maps to itself with {3,6}
exchanged. On the twelve-position ring the analogous negation fixes exactly
two positions, 0 and 6.

**Rotation r and mirror s on any ring satisfy the dihedral relation s r s = r⁻¹: conjugating a rotation by the mirror reverses it.** Rotation
and reflection fail to commute for every rotation of order greater than
two, and the failure of commutation is precisely the existence of the
reversal. The relation defines the dihedral groups; the smallest
non-abelian group is D₃ ≅ S₃, of order six, the symmetry group of the
triad, and the structure is native to the figure.†

<div class="vnote">† Version 3 called the relation the smallest
noncommutative structure in mathematics. The smallest non-abelian group
is D₃, of order six.</div>

**An odd mirror-symmetric figure with a root cannot contain its own antipode: the mirror fixes the root, occupied, and the antipodal position, necessarily empty.** On the nine-point figure
these are the root 9 and the empty midpoint of the 4–5 gap.

## 3 · The cycle: the period of 1/7 as rotation of the remainders

**Definition: for m coprime to the base B, the cycle of m in base B is the orbit of the base's powers among the residues modulo m.**
It is the sequence 1, B, B², … (mod m), which is the cyclic group
generated by B in the multiplicative group of residues. It comes with a
digit realization — the repeating block of 1/m, whose successive
rotations are the repeating blocks of the successive remainders. Its
*period* is the multiplicative order of B modulo m. Advancing the
expansion one place multiplies the remainder by B: one place is one
rotation.

**The master cycle is m = 7 in base B = 10, whose reptend is 142857.** The period is ord₇(10) = 6 = 7 − 1, the maximum, which is what *full reptend*
means; the remainders cycle 1, 3, 2, 6, 4, 5; the digit set is exactly
the hexad.

**Each place of the expansion of 1/7 multiplies the remainder by three, since 10 ≡ 3 (mod 7).**
10³ ≡ −1 (mod 7): three places effect negation. 10⁶ ≡ 1: six places close the
cycle, and the closure returns short — 10⁶ − 7 × 142857 = 1. The remainder
that ends the cycle is the seed that restarts it.

**The k-th multiple of the reptend is a cyclic rotation of it, which is the cyclic number property.** And k × 142857 × 7
= k·10⁶ − k exactly, for k = 1…6: the absolute shortfall is k parts, and
the relative shortfall 10⁻⁶ for every k. The cycle reads totals and
cannot read factorizations: the relative shortfall of k = 4 = 2² equals
that of k = 6 = 2·3 exactly.

**The full reptend primes, those for which ten is a primitive root, begin 7, 17, 19, 23, 29, 47, 59, 61, 97.** Seven is the least, and the only
one below the base.

## 4 · The two orders and the line theorem

**Definition: the positional order is the digit sequence of the reptend, 1, 4, 2, 8, 5, 7.**

**Definition: the multiplicative order is the doubling orbit modulo nine, 1 → 2 → 4 → 8 → 7 → 5 → 1, the cycle written 124875.**

**The line theorem. The remainder path of 1/p in base b, the successive remainders b^k mod p, is a single closed stroke through distinct residues if and only if the reptend is full.** The digits of the
block are the remainders' images and may repeat.† Equivalently, the
number of reptend classes (p − 1)/ord_p(b) equals one, which is to say b
is a primitive root modulo p. Seven is the least prime for which base
ten draws one line. Thirteen requires two strokes (076923 and 153846);
eleven requires five. For the pair (7, 10) the one closed line carries
five things at once. It carries the state space, which is the residues.
It carries the dynamics, since the line is the orbit of the shift, and
the symmetry, since its point reflection is Midy's theorem. It carries
the conservation structure, the untouched triangle 3-6-9. And it carries
the first operation, the involution between its two Hamiltonian
readings, 142857 and 124875.

<div class="vnote">† Version 3 said the digit path passes through
distinct points. The remainders are distinct; the digits are not, and
1/7 is the one full-reptend prime below 130 whose digits are.</div>

**The hexad path is unicursal, one closed stroke through six distinct points, and its complement among the nine residues is exactly the triad, the axis the line never touches.**

The stroke count is (p−1)/ord_p(10). Seven draws one line and is the least prime that does.</div></div>

## 5 · Midy and the two families: complementary halves, closure and negation

**Midy's theorem (1836). If 1/q, with q prime, has even period 2h in base B, the two halves of the repeating block sum digitwise to B − 1.**
For 1/7: 142 + 857 = 999, at every place. Equivalently: B^h ≡ −1 (mod
q), the half-period shift is negation, and the figure's point reflection
is this theorem drawn. For a composite modulus the property is exactly
that q divides B^h + 1, as the negation family below states.†

<div class="vnote">† Version 3 stated the theorem for any modulus of
even period. It holds for primes and prime powers; for 1/21 the halves
sum to 666.</div>

**For a prime modulus of even period 2h, the block's digits sum to 9h and the completion to 9·2h, the digit-sum consequence of Midy's theorem.** The
master cycle sums to 27 with completion 54; the cycle of 137 (period 8)
sums to 36 with completion 72; the ratio of the two completions is 72/54
= 4/3.

**Every even cycle's modulus factors as closure times negation: B^{2h} − 1 = (B^h − 1)(B^h + 1).** In base ten the closure family is the all-nines integers 9,
99, 999, …; the negation family is 11, 101, 1001, 10001, … Midy's
theorem is exactly membership in the negation family: q divides B^h + 1.
Residents: 1001 = 7 × 11 × 13; 10001 = 73 × 137; 10⁸ + 1 = 17 × 5882353.

**Because 99 × 101 = 9999, the reciprocals of 99 and 101 write each other forever.** 1/99 = 0.010101… and 1/101 = 0.00990099…: over four places each is the other's repeating block.

**The two families floor at two and at zero.** Descending the negation family's rungs 10² + 1, 10¹ + 1, 10⁰ + 1 gives 101, 11, 2: the family floors at two. The closure family floors at 10⁰ − 1 = 0.

**The cycle of 137 has exactly two palindromic rotations, at the residues +10 and −10, half a period apart, carried onto each other by the Midy half-turn; the master cycle has none.** The
−10 face is 92700729 = 127 × 9 × 11 × 73 × 101, and 127 = 2⁷ − 1 with
137 − 127 = 10.

**One product carries the four moduli: 99999999 = 99 × 101 × 73 × 137.** The factors are the two-place closure, its negation partner, and the Midy pair of 137. Every rotation of the 137 cycle is a multiple
of 729927 = 9 × 11 × 73 × 101.

## 6 · The gallery of multiplication: times-k diagrams and their epicycloids

**Definition: the times-k diagram on a circle of N points joins each point n to kn mod N by a chord.** The diagram depends on k and N; its envelope — the
curve the chords outline — depends on k alone, with N only refining it.

**The epicycloid theorem. The envelope of the times-k diagram is the epicycloid with k − 1 cusps.** It is the cardioid at k = 2, the nephroid
at k = 3, the three-cusped epicycloid at k = 4, the four-cusped at k =
5, and so on. Machine checked at modulus 720 for k = 2 through 5.

**The envelope touches the base circle at the fixed points, and the cusps
stand between them.** From kθ ≡ θ comes (k − 1)θ ≡ 0, so the map θ ↦ kθ
has exactly k − 1 fixed points, at θⱼ = 2πj/(k − 1). There the chord
degenerates to a point and the envelope touches the base circle,
smoothly. The k − 1 cusps stand midway between the fixed points, at (2j +
1)π/(k − 1), where kθ ≡ θ + π and the chord is a diameter. They lie on
the circle of radius (k − 1)/(k + 1), which is one third for the
cardioid. The two counts agree: as many cusps as the multiplier leaves
fixed.†

<div class="vnote">† Version 3 placed the cusps at the fixed points,
where the envelope touches the base circle. The contact there is a
smooth tangency; the cusps stand midway between, on the circle of radius
(k − 1)/(k + 1). The count k − 1 is unchanged, and Plate III now marks
the cusps.</div>

The envelope carries k−1 cusps and depends on the multiplier alone. Red marks the cusps, on the circle of radius (k−1)/(k+1); the envelope touches the base circle between them, at the fixed points of the map.</div></div>

**As N grows, the discrete envelope converges monotonically on the smooth epicycloid.** Measured as the greatest distance from the envelope's points to the curve, the
cardioid's deviation at modulus 60 is 2.7 parts in a thousand of the
radius. It shrinks to 1.7 parts in ten thousand at 240 and 1.1 parts in
a hundred thousand at 960; the higher epicycloids converge at the same
rate from a larger start.† The smooth curve is the diagram's
large-modulus limit — a figure the counting construction approaches and
never is. Every drawn epicycloid in this gallery is a finite diagram;
the continuum curve is its limit.

<div class="vnote">† Version 3 gave four parts in a thousand at modulus
60 for the family. That holds for the cardioid; the nephroid and the
three- and four-cusped curves sit at four to seven parts in a thousand
there.</div>

**The figure's multiplicative line 124875 is the doubling orbit modulo nine, so the enneagram's line is six of the seven chords of the times-two diagram at modulus nine, the seventh joining 3 and 6.** Nine is the least modulus at which the doubling
orbit of 1 has length six. The larger instances refine the same curve:
modulus 10, and modulus 49, where the doubling orbit runs its full
ord₄₉(2) = 21. The gallery places the figure in an infinite family: one
multiplier, one curve, every modulus a resolution of it.

**The base acts as identity on the ennead.** 10 ≡ 1 (mod 9): the decad
is the monad's return. That is why digit sums live on the nine ring, and
why the times-ten diagram at modulus nine draws no curve at all — the
base's own map fixes every point of the ennead.

## 7 · The ladder of best approximation: the convergents of log₂3 and the commas

**2^p = 3^q has no solution in positive integers — an even number never equals an odd one — so the ratio log₂3 is irrational and the mutual rotation of the two generators never closes.**

**The convergents of log₂3 alternate sides strictly and forever.** The continued fraction begins [1; 1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 1, 5, …], with convergents 1/1, 2/1, 3/2, 8/5, 19/12, 65/41, 84/53, 485/306 and so on.

**The first five convergents miss by classical intervals, and the sides alternate between the three-heavy and the two-heavy.** The stations are 3/2 (the fifth), 4/3 (the fourth), 9/8 (the whole tone),
256/243 (the limma), 531441/524288 (the Pythagorean comma), the
41-comma, the 53-comma. The record minima of |q·log₂3 − p| fall exactly
at the convergent denominators 1, 2, 5, 12, 41, 53, 306 and so on.

**The first four convergents — 1/1, 2/1, 3/2, 8/5 — are all pairs of consecutive Fibonacci numbers; the ladder leaves the golden staircase at 19/12, the comma rung.** The semiconvergent preceding
8/5 is 5/3, the mediant of 2/1 and 3/2, lying between 8/5 and 2/1; whose
station is 2⁵/3³ = 32/27.

**The directed four-vector of a pair (n, n+1) — difference, the pair, sum — totals 4n + 3.** For (2,3): (1, 2, 3, 5), four
consecutive Fibonacci numbers, total 11 = 10 + 1, the negation family's
first rung. For (3,4): (1, 3, 4, 7), four consecutive Lucas numbers, total
15 = 3 × 5.

**The reversal chain rises locally forever and cycles globally, over by the comma.** Iterating "the fifth's reversal becomes the new
fifth" does three things. It climbs the tonic by 9/8 per step in the
three-limit, exactly. It carries a slip of exactly 81/80 when the
five-limit value is used. And it closes six steps at one octave times
the Pythagorean comma: (9/8)⁶ = 2 × 531441/524288.

**The syntonic comma 81/80 is the gap between four just fifths reduced and the just third; the Pythagorean comma 531441/524288 is the gap between twelve fifths and seven octaves.** It is equally the gap between six whole tones and the octave. The Pythagorean comma is a consequence of §7's first
entry, no stack of fifths meeting a stack of octaves; the syntonic comma
needs as well that 3⁴ ≠ 2⁴ · 5, the independence of the third prime.

## 8 · The rings and the scales: transposition, reflection and the step words

**Definition: the transpositional stabilizer of a subset S of the ring ℤ/12 is {t : S + t = S}.** A set fixed by a nonzero shift cannot
distinguish a candidate root from its shift: interior evidence is invariant.

**The diatonic set {0,2,4,5,7,9,11} and the pentatonic {0,2,4,7,9} have trivial stabilizer.** The whole tone set is fixed by six
shifts, the octatonic by four, the full chromatic by twelve: the sets of
limited transposition.

**The diatonic step word 2 2 1 2 2 2 1 has exactly two unit steps, at positions 4–5 and 11–0.** The whole tone word
2 2 2 2 2 2 has none.

**The reflection x ↦ 7 − x maps the diatonic set on 0 to its parallel minor {0,2,3,5,7,8,10}.** It carries the four-note chord {7,11,2,5} to
{0,8,5,2}. The reflection through the root, x ↦ −x, applied to the first
three prime partials of a fundamental exchanges the upward reduction {1,
5/4, 3/2} with the downward {1, 4/3, 8/5}. The reflection x ↦ 7 − x
carries the major triad to the minor, {1, 6/5, 3/2}.† Each downward
address is exactly the octave over its upward partner: 4/3 = 2 ÷ 3/2 and
8/5 = 2 ÷ 5/4.

<div class="vnote">† Version 3 said the same reflection, x ↦ 7 − x,
exchanges the two reductions. It is x ↦ −x; x ↦ 7 − x gives the minor
triad.</div>

**On the lattice with fundamental 24, 45 → 48 and 32 → 30 are both moves of the ratio 16/15, in opposite directions, using +3 and −2 — one unit of each generator.** The two moves turn the
interval 45/32 into 48/30 = 8/5.

## 9 · The spectrum of the figure: the normal frequencies of the nine-cycle

**Definition: the quadratic form is the sum of squared differences around the nine-cycle, the Dirichlet form.** It is even under global sign change, E(−u) = E(u), and
mirror invariant.

**The form's normal frequencies are 2 sin(20k°) for k = 0…4: one zero, the uniform mode, and four doubled positives.** Their values are 0.6840…, 1.2855…, 1.7320… = √3 and 1.9696…. The four positives are the roots of the
integer quartic x⁴ − 9x³ + 27x² − 30x + 9 = 0 taken as squared
frequencies, machine checked to twelve digits. The quartic factors as (x
− 3)(x³ − 6x² + 9x − 3): the squared frequency 3 is rational, and the
other three are conjugates under an irreducible cubic.

**The mirror splits each doubled frequency into an even and an odd partner.** The zero mode is even; the odd sector — the pair
sector — contains no zero mode, and its spectrum begins at the strictly
positive 2 sin 20°.

**Every odd moment of every linear observable vanishes identically in any ensemble weighted by an even function of the configuration.** Such an ensemble is closed under global sign change with equal weights. This is verified by full enumeration over the 3⁹
configurations u in {−1, 0, +1}⁹, a set closed under negation, in exact
rational arithmetic: the skew is zero, not small.

**Identification: the figure's pair layer carries the primordial fluctuations, and under that identification
primordial non-Gaussianity of the local kind is forbidden.** The identification is a physical hypothesis and does not
follow from the theorem above. It places the two-signed fluctuations of the early universe in the odd sector of the form
and the one-signed mean, the vacuum term, in the even sector. Its transfer to the measured curvature perturbation runs
through one further step, the join of the ladder in the time direction, which is itself an identification. Under both,
the intrinsic skew of the primordial field is zero, and so is f_NL, the amplitude of the quadratic term in the standard
local parametrization. Single-field slow-roll inflation predicts a local amplitude of the order of the spectral tilt,
about one hundredth [Maldacena 2003], so the two accounts agree in kind; multi-field models permit an amplitude of order
one or larger. A confirmed primordial local f_NL of a few or more refutes the identification and leaves the theorem
standing. Planck's combined temperature and polarization analysis gives f_NL = −0.9 ± 5.1 at 68 percent confidence
[Planck Collaboration 2020], and the SPHEREx all-sky survey is designed to reach |f_NL| < 1 at two standard deviations
[Doré et al. 2014]. Whether the one-signed mean couples at second order and returns a small skew of computable sign
remains open. The identification may or may not be physics in terms of our current understanding, and the measurement
decides it.

## 10 · The cyclotomic tower: binary closures factored by depth

**The binary all-ones factors exactly over its divisor depths: 2^d − 1 = ∏ Φ_e(2) over the divisors e of d, where Φ is the cyclotomic polynomial.** Each Φ_e(2) carries a prime of exact
order e, except at e = 6, where Φ₆(2) = 3 repeats Φ₂(2).

**Iterating "the value of the closure becomes the next depth" from 2 gives 2 → 3 → 7 → 127 → 2¹²⁷ − 1, the sequence of Catalan and Mersenne.** The cyclotomic values are Φ₂(2) = 3, Φ₃(2) = 7 and Φ₇(2) = 127 = 2⁷ − 1, and every member is prime as far as verified.

**Depths 3 and 5 first meet at depth 15: 2¹⁵ − 1 = 7 × 31 × 151.** Φ₁₅(2) = 151 is prime and ord₂(151) = 15. The prime 151 is the
novel content of the first joint closure of the three-cycle and the
five-cycle.

**A cycle of period D contains every divisor-period cycle inside it: d divides D exactly when 2^d − 1 divides 2^D − 1.**

## 11 · The register: the bases in which the construction runs

**Two conditions must hold for the construction to run in base B.** The ennead
must live in digit sums, which requires B ≡ 1 (mod 9). And the seed must
unroll to full period, which requires B to be a primitive root modulo 7 — that
is, B ≡ 3 or 5 (mod 7).

**By the Chinese Remainder Theorem the carriers are exactly B ≡ 10 or 19 (mod 63).** They are the bases 10, 19, 73, 82, 136, 145, …, and ten is the least.

**The base is the ennead's own return, 10 ≡ 1 (mod 9): the tenth position is the first position an octave of the ring later.** The condition B ≡ 1 (mod 9) says exactly that a carrier base must be
a decad of some ennead: the completion that returns.

**The period of 1/7 is six in every carrier base, and Midy's complementation holds in every base with even period.**
Likewise the one-unit residue of each complementary pair is base-free;
the digit strings themselves, and which depths carry which signs, are
the base's own. The counts are invariant; the labels are not.


## 12 · The sixty forms and their twelve classes

The figure's six moving points are the units of the integers modulo nine, 1, 2, 4, 5, 7 and 8, a cyclic group of order six
generated by doubling. The remaining residues 3, 6 and 9 form the triangle, which the moving path never enters.

**The six units admit exactly sixty closed forms.** A closed form is a cyclic sequence through all six units. The 720 orderings
divide by six rotations and by two directions, and 720/12 = 60. Every closed figure that can be drawn through the six points is one
of these sixty.

**The mirror sorts the sixty into three classes.** The mirror sends each digit to its nine-complement, d to 9 − d, which is
multiplication by eight. Four forms are balanced: opposite positions sum to nine, and each form is its own mirror. Twelve are
harmonic: each is its own mirror by reversal without being balanced. Forty-four are chiral, in twenty-two mirror pairs. Exactly one
form, 124578, is convex, and it is harmonic.

| class | count | property |
|---|---|---|
| **balanced** | **4** | opposite positions sum to nine; each is its own mirror |
| **harmonic** | **12** | its own mirror by reversal, without being balanced |
| **chiral** | **44** | not its own mirror; twenty-two left/right pairs |

The whole catalogue is drawn overleaf on a single sheet, banded by class: the
four balanced, the twelve harmonic, the forty-four chiral. It is the entire
vocabulary of the figure at modulus nine, and it fits on one page.

style="font-size:6.8pt;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:3px">PLATE IV — the complete catalogue: nothing omitted, nothing repeated. Sixteen of the sixty are self-mirror, one is convex, and both traditional readings of the figure are balanced. Point labels are dropped in the chiral band alone, where the ring is read from the bands above.</div></div>

**The figure's arithmetic has twelve symmetries.** Multiplying every digit by a unit, or replacing every digit by a unit divided by
it, sends closed forms to closed forms. These twelve maps are the symmetries of the doubling hexagon 1, 2, 4, 8, 7, 5: in the
logarithm to base two they are its six rotations and six reflections. They split as the mirror times the six maps that keep each
residue class modulo three, the residue-one class {1, 4, 7} and the residue-two class {2, 5, 8}.

**Theorem 12.1. The twelve symmetries sort the sixty forms into exactly twelve classes.** Their sizes are 1, 2, 3, 3, 3, 6, 6, 6,
6, 6, 6 and 12. Each class lies inside one of the mirror's classes: the balanced forms make two classes, the harmonic forms three
and the chiral forms seven. A class counts the forms that the figure's own arithmetic cannot tell apart.

**The doubling form is the only form that every symmetry fixes.** It is the class of size one, 124875, which the record names
matter. The balanced forms 142857, 147852 and 152847, named rest, force and spine, make one class of three: digit by digit, force
is four times rest and spine is twice rest, modulo nine. Read as the multiplier taken at each step, matter is doubling six times,
and rest, force and spine are one word, ×4, ×5, ×4 repeated, begun at three different points.

**Two quantities sort the classes further.** The first is the residue pattern, the sequence of residue classes around a form. It
alternates on 6 forms, falls into two blocks of three on 18, and is mixed on 36. A mixed form has exactly one pair of adjacent
residue-one digits and one pair of adjacent residue-two digits. The second is the number of complement chords, the chords that
join a digit to its nine-complement. It runs from zero to three.

**Theorem 12.2. Each class has its own address.** The residue pattern, the number of complement chords and the symmetries that fix
a form are the same for every member of a class, and no two classes share all three. The pattern and the chord count alone
separate ten classes. Two cells of the pattern-by-count grid are empty for a reason: an alternating form cannot have exactly one
complement chord, and a blocked form cannot have three.

| representative | size | residue pattern | complement chords | fixed by |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 124875 (matter) | 1 | alternating | 0 | all twelve symmetries |
| 127548 | 2 | alternating | 3 | the class rotations and each mirror-times-inversion |
| 124578 (convex) | 3 | alternating | 2 | the mirror and one inversion |
| 142857 (rest) | 3 | blocked | 0 | the mirror and one inversion |
| 145827 | 3 | blocked | 2 | the mirror and one inversion |
| 124785 | 6 | mixed | 0 | one mirror-times-inversion |
| 125478 | 6 | mixed | 2 | the mirror alone |
| 125748 | 6 | mixed | 1 | one mirror-times-inversion |
| 125847 | 6 | blocked | 0 | one mirror-times-inversion |
| 127458 | 6 | mixed | 3 | one mirror-times-inversion |
| 128547 | 6 | blocked | 1 | one mirror-times-inversion |
| 124587 | 12 | mixed | 1 | no symmetry |

Here an inversion is a map d to u/d with u in the residue-one class, and a mirror-times-inversion is an inversion followed by the
mirror. The class rotations are multiplication by 4 and by 7.

**Every class has a structural reason, read through the nine-complement.** The reason differs with the residue pattern.

*The alternating forms are the six pairings of the two residue classes.* An alternating form uses six of the nine chords between
the residue classes and leaves out three, which pair the residue-one digits with the residue-two digits. Read through the
nine-complement, the pairing is a permutation of {1, 4, 7}. Matter leaves out the identity. The class of 127548, made of 127548 and
154278, leaves out the two 3-cycles. The convex class, made of 124578, 127845 and 157248, leaves out the three transpositions. So the
alternating classes of sizes one, two and three are the conjugacy classes of the symmetric group on three letters.

*The blocked forms are fixed by their middles and their joins.* A blocked form is a block of three residue-one digits joined at
both ends to a block of three residue-two digits. Its classes differ in whether the middle digits of the two blocks are
nine-complements, and in how many of the two joins are complement chords. Rest's class has complementary middles and no
complementary join. The class of 145827 has complementary middles and both joins. The class of 125847 has neither, and the class of
128547 has one join.

*The mixed forms are fixed by which of their links are complement chords.* Each mixed form reads, in exactly one rotation and
direction, as two residue-two digits, one residue-one digit, one residue-two digit and two residue-one digits, closed back to the
start. Its four links between the residue classes are the middle, two inner joins and the outer join. No link is a complement chord
in the class of 124785; the middle alone in the class of 125748; the middle and the outer join in the class of 125478. One inner join
is a complement chord in the class of 124587, which is why that class has twice the size and no symmetry. Both inner joins and the
outer join are complement chords in the class of 127458. No other combination occurs, because three complement pairs force one
another.

## 13 · The transforms of the figure: the first operation and the involutions

A transform is a permutation of the six units, and it carries each closed form to another. The first operation of the companion
record on the origin is one: digit by digit it carries the doubling form 124875 to the positional form 142857, exchanging 2 with 4
and 5 with 7 and holding 1 and 8 fixed.

**Each of the two orders is the orbit of one multiplier in its own modulus.** The doubling order is doubling modulo nine, acting on
the digits. The positional order is multiplication by ten modulo seven, acting on the remainders of the division by seven, carried
to the digits by r to ⌊10r/7⌋. Stepped in the other modulus, each order moves +2, −1, +2, +2, −1, +2 in the logarithm, and the
correspondence between remainders and digits, read in logarithms, is the first operation itself.

**The first operation exchanges two alternations.** On the doubling order the residue classes alternate: the squares modulo nine,
{1, 4, 7}, against {2, 5, 8}. On the positional order the alternation is {1, 2, 5} against {4, 7, 8}, the images of the quadratic
residues modulo seven. The first operation carries each alternation to the other.

**Result 13.1. Up to the figure's symmetries there are twelve transforms out of the doubling form.** Two transforms that differ by a
symmetry applied before or after are one operation seen in another frame. Because the twelve symmetries are exactly the doubling
form's own, these frames sort the transforms out of the doubling form by the class of the form they reach, the twelve classes of §12.

**Theorem 13.2. The first operation is the only one of the eleven non-trivial transforms that commutes with the mirror.** A transform
out of the doubling form commutes with the mirror exactly when it keeps the pairs of nine-complements, and that happens exactly
when the form it reaches is balanced. The balanced forms make two classes: the doubling form's own, and the class of rest, force and
spine, which the first operation reaches.

**Result 13.3. The transforms that keep the residue classes are exactly those onto the alternating forms.** A transform keeps the
residue classes when it sends the residue-one digits into one class and the residue-two digits into the other. The first operation
does not: it carries the alternating residue pattern of the doubling form to the blocked pattern of rest.

**Result 13.4. Four classes contain no transform that is its own inverse.** Every other class contains an involution, as the first
operation is one. The four are the classes of 125478, 125847, 127458 and 128547, and each has six members.

**Theorem 13.5. On the four interior units the mirror, the inversion and the first operation are one Klein four-group.** On {2, 4, 5,
7} the mirror, the inversion d to 1/d and the first operation are the three involutions of the group, each the product of the other
two. On the whole hexad the mirror equals the exchange of 1 and 8 composed with the other two. Of the three, only the inversion keeps
each residue class.

**Theorem 13.6. Of the figure's seven involutive symmetries, only the three inversions keep each residue class.** The seven are the
mirror, the three inversions d to u/d and the three mirror-times-inversions. The three inversions are conjugate under the class
rotations, and they do not commute with them. On the doubling hexagon, where the twelve symmetries act as the rotations and reflections of a regular hexagon, the three inversions are the reflections in the three lines through opposite vertices, and the mirror is the central reflection. Read on the weights of the two triplets of SU(3), which form the same hexagon, the three inversions are the reflections of the Weyl group of the root system A₂.

**The residue patterns count the complete pairings a form contains.** Among a form's chords between the residue classes, an
alternating form contains two complete pairings of the residue-one digits with the residue-two digits, a mixed form one, and a
blocked form none. Read through the nine-complement, each pairing is a permutation of {1, 4, 7}. An alternating form's two pairings
and the one it leaves out make up all nine chords between the classes, in one of two ways: the identity with the two 3-cycles, or
the three transpositions.

**Theorem 13.7. A mirror-times-inversion fixes a mixed form exactly when the pairing it contains is even.** Such a symmetry can fix a
mixed form only by reversing it, residue-two pair for residue-one pair and single for single. The pairing it induces is the
contained pairing with its two inner residue-two digits exchanged, of the opposite sign. The three mirror-times-inversions induce
exactly the three odd pairings. So the symmetry fixes the form exactly when the contained pairing is even.

## 14 · The modulus expanded: orbit figures and chord diagrams beyond nine

Every figure here is drawn on a circle of N points. This section asks what
changes when N does, and separates two objects that the single word *figure*
has been carrying.

**Definition: the orbit figure traces 1 → k → k² → … and closes when it returns; the chord diagram joins every point n to kn.** The enneagram's line is the orbit figure of k = 2, N = 9. The orbit figure is a
stroke and may fail to close; the chord diagram is a field of chords and
always draws. They coincide on the units when the orbit exhausts the
units; at modulus nine the chord diagram adds the one chord 3–6 to the
line, and above nine the distinction is decisive.

**Closure is coprimality.** The orbit closes into one cycle from its first step
if and only if gcd(k, N) = 1. Otherwise it runs down a **tail** into a shorter
cycle: a flow into a loop, not a closed stroke.

| N | factors | units | gcd(2,N)=1 | doubling orbit of 1 | closed forms on the units |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 9 | 3² | 6 | yes | cycle 6 | 60 |
| 24 | 2³·3 | 8 | **no** | tail 3 → cycle 2 | 2,520 |
| 99 | 3²·11 | 60 | yes | cycle 30 | an 80-digit number |
| 1008 | 2⁴·3²·7 | 288 | **no** | tail 4 → cycle 6 | a 583-digit number |

**The catalogue is drawable only where the units number six.**
Twenty-four already carries 2,520 closed forms; ninety-nine carries an
eighty-digit number of them and one thousand and eight a
five-hundred-and-eighty-three-digit number. At N = 7, 14 and 18 the same
sixty forms recur, nine being the one of the four at which doubling
generates the units. Where the units exceed six the catalogue is
enumerated by class, never by exhaustion, and the classes persist:
twenty-four has 24 balanced forms and 120 self-mirror among its 2,520,
and **exactly one convex form**, as every modulus does.

style="flex:1 1 100%;font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:0.5em;text-align:center">PLATE V — the doubling orbit at four moduli. Red is cycle, black is tail. Nine and ninety-nine are odd and the stroke closes; twenty-four and one thousand and eight are even and it collapses. Raising N does not repair this — the obstruction is a shared factor, not a resolution.</div></div>

**The repair is a different multiplier.** Five is the smallest multiplier
coprime to all four moduli, and the fivefold orbit closes at every one of them.
The modulus does not forbid a stroke; it forbids *that* stroke.

100%;font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:0.5em;text-align:center">PLATE VI — the fivefold orbit at the same four moduli. All four close.</div></div>

**The envelope's symmetry belongs to the multiplier.** The envelope's
cusp count is k − 1 and nothing else. The cardioid is one-cusped and has
a single mirror axis at *every* modulus, never a rotation; raising N
refines the drawing of the curve. The drawn diagram's own rotational
symmetry appears exactly when k − 1 divides N, as the next paragraph
states. What increases with N otherwise is resolution — forty degrees
between points at nine, and about a third of a degree at one thousand
and eight.

style="font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:-0.35em">N=1008</div></div><div style="flex:1 1 100%;font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:0.5em;text-align:center">PLATE VII — the times-two chord diagram at the same four moduli. Nothing breaks: the cardioid is present throughout and its single cusp sits on the zero at every modulus.</div></div>

**The modulus decides whether a cusp is drawn.** The continuous map θ ↦
kθ has k − 1 fixed points; the lattice map n ↦ kn mod N has gcd(k − 1,
N). These agree — so the cusps stand on points the figure actually draws
— precisely when **k − 1 divides N**. Otherwise only gcd(k − 1, N) of
the k − 1 cusps stand on points, the one at θ = 0 among them, and the
rest fall between.

**A highly composite modulus is the richer host, because the gallery lands on the lattice once per divisor of N.** Of the multipliers up to twenty-five,
nine realises three exactly, ninety-nine four, twenty-four eight, and
one thousand and eight fourteen — the number of divisors of N not
exceeding twenty-four. Twenty-four is a richer host than ninety-nine
despite being smaller. The cardioid alone is universal, since one
divides every modulus.

**Taking the eight positions of the octave as moduli, only two host a closed doubling figure — and they are the two odd positions, Re at 27 and Si at 45.**

| position | Do | Re | Mi | Fa | Sol | La | Si | Do′ |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| modulus | 24 | **27** | 30 | 32 | 36 | 40 | **45** | 48 |
| doubling closes | no | **yes** | no | no | no | no | **yes** | no |

Re's orbit runs eighteen points and Si's twelve, both closed. Doubling
closes at N exactly when N is odd, so the two hosts are the two odd
positions, 27 and 45, which are for the same reason the only two that do
not halve into integers.†

<div class="vnote">† Version 3 counted three independent conditions
selecting the pair, among them a three in the ratio's denominator.
Closure under doubling and failure to halve are one condition, oddness;
the denominator condition picks out Fa and La.</div>

## 15 · The five structured forms: balance as divisibility by 999

Five of the sixty carry structure: the four balanced forms and the one
convex. Read as six-digit numbers, **the four balanced forms are exactly
the multiples of 999** — and the convex form is exactly the one that is
not. Balance and divisibility by 999 are two names for one property,
opposite digits summing to nine; self-mirror symmetry is the wider class
of sixteen, which adds the twelve harmonic forms.†

<div class="vnote">† Version 3 made self-mirror symmetry a third name
for the same property. Sixteen forms are self-mirror and four are
balanced, as §12 states.</div>

| form | name | index | = 999 × | crossings | character |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **142857** | rest | 143 | 11·13 | 3 | the reptend of 1/7 |
| **124875** | matter | 125 | 5³ | 1 | pure doubling |
| **147852** | force | 148 | 2²·37 | 3 | the triad-cosets |
| **152847** | spine | 153 | 3²·17 | 7 | the antipode-fold |
| **124578** | harmonic | — | — | 0 | the convex near-line |

Every form sums to 27, digital root nine. The crossing counts below are
recomputed here by segment intersection and agree with the book's.

100%;font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:0.5em;text-align:center">**REST** — the reptend of 1/7. The figure as traditionally drawn: the digits of one seventh in the order they appear. Its index 143 is eleven times thirteen — the two primes whose product with seven gives 1001, the repunit that makes the six-digit period possible at all.</div></div>

**Its six rotations are the six sevenths themselves** — one repeating block, six starting points, six fractions. Midy's theorem seals the halves, 142 + 857 = 999, and that law drawn as geometry is precisely the reflection exchanging each point with its nine-complement. Rest is its own mirror for that reason, as every balanced form is. Whether infinitely many primes join seven in this property is Artin's conjecture, open for a century: the figure sits one instance inside an unsettled question.

dominant-baseline="middle" fill="#000000" font-family="DIN Alternate,Avenir Next,sans-serif">7</text><text x="-55.2" y="-65.8" font-size="12.7" text-anchor="middle" dominant-baseline="middle" fill="#000000" font-family="DIN Alternate,Avenir Next,sans-serif">8</text></svg><div style="font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:-0.35em">124875</div></div><div style="flex:1 1 100%;font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:0.5em;text-align:center">**MATTER** — pure doubling. The only form of the sixty that steps by a constant multiplier: each point is twice the last, modulo nine. Its index 125 is five cubed, and it crosses itself once — the least of any balanced form.</div></div>

**What the doubling builds, it keeps.** Continued past the six positions
— 16, 32, 64, 128, 256, 512 — the series never leaves the orbit, because
doubling modulo nine has period six and nothing escapes it. The same six
digits also index two ladders running in opposite directions: one
ascending by doubling from twenty-four, one descending one at a time
from 207 to 202. Two hundred seven is formed from the hexad's two
half-sums, {1,2,4} to seven and {8,7,5} to twenty, written larger first
as 20|7. At the digit five the two ladders read 768 and 206; set side by
side that is the muon in electron masses to three decimals. Of the six
digits, five is the one whose two readings do this. The construction is
exact and checkable by hand; two choices were made, the order of the
half-sums and the laying of the ladders alongside each other, and what
it means for particle physics is open.

fill="#000000" font-family="DIN Alternate,Avenir Next,sans-serif">8</text></svg><div style="font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:-0.35em">147852</div></div><div style="flex:1 1 100%;font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:0.5em;text-align:center">**FORCE** — the triad-cosets. The form whose steps run through the cosets of the triad. Its index 148 is four times thirty-seven, and thirty-seven is the prime that turns three repeated digits into a multiple of 999.</div></div>

**Its two multiplications are the cycle's own rotations.** Multiplying
every digit by four advances it two places along the doubling cycle
124875; by seven, back two. On the hexagon of the six movers in doubling
order those are exactly the two rotations through 120 degrees. Force is
four such steps joined by two single-place steps.† Each multiplication
carries its own limiting curve: times-four is tangent to a three-cusped
curve, times-seven to a six-cusped one — m − 1 cusps for every
multiplier m. And 37, in its index, is the prime of the nines: 27 × 37 =
999, the number that divides every balanced form.

<div class="vnote">† Version 3 said multiplication by four advances
every digit three positions on the circle. It does so for 1, 4 and 7 and
advances 2, 5 and 8 by six; the uniform description is along the
doubling cycle, two places.</div>

fill="#000000" font-family="DIN Alternate,Avenir Next,sans-serif">8</text></svg><div style="font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:-0.35em">152847</div></div><div style="flex:1 1 100%;font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:0.5em;text-align:center">**SPINE** — the antipode-fold. The most self-crossing form of the sixty, at seven crossings. Its index 153 is nine times seventeen, where seventeen is three to the fourth less two to the sixth — the closest approach of the two ladders beyond the unit — and 153 is also the seventeenth triangular number.</div></div>

**Multiplication by eight is multiplication by minus one**, since eight
and nine differ by one — so it is the complement map itself, every digit
sent to nine minus itself, which is to say the mirror. The reflection
that defines left and right on this circle is one of the cycle's own
multiplications, and Spine is the form built from it. Its limiting curve
carries seven cusps, the most of any multiplier here. The axis 1–8 is
also the axis the involution between the two readings holds still. A
form built around the one pairing the transform leaves fixed is the most
tangled of the four when drawn: stability at the center and complexity
at the rim, one fact seen twice.

fill="#000000" font-family="DIN Alternate,Avenir Next,sans-serif">8</text></svg><div style="font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:-0.35em">124578</div></div><div style="flex:1 1 100%;font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:0.5em;text-align:center">**HARMONIC** — the convex near-line. The six units in their own circular order: the simplest closed hexagon that can be drawn on them, and the only form of the sixty that never crosses itself. It is not balanced — its opposite pairs do not sum to nine — but it is its own mirror, because reversing it gives its own nine-complement.</div></div>

**Its reversal is its own complement.** Reversed, 124578 reads 875421, which is digit for digit the nine-complement of the original — and a form whose reversal is its complement is its own mirror for that reason. It is the convex representative of the twelve harmonic forms, the only symmetry class outside balance. The same ordering has a second and independent source. It is exactly the order
in which the six digits appear as the *leading* figure of the six sevenths:
.142857, .285714, .428571, .571428, .714285, .857142, read down their first
column, give 1, 2, 4, 5, 7, 8. Two unrelated questions — one about self-intersection, one about leading digits — are answered by one string.

## 16 · The intonated layout: one figure measured by count and by interval

Everything above concerns the figure's **ordering** — which points join to
which. This section concerns its **layout** — where the points sit. The two are
independent, and the distinction is the volume's sharpest: the combinatorial
object cannot see how the points are placed, and all the metric structure lives
in the placement.

dominant-baseline="middle" fill="#000000" font-family="DIN Alternate,Avenir Next,sans-serif">8</text></svg><div style="font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:-0.35em">tuned — five of the nine unmoved</div></div><div style="flex:1 1 100%;font-size:0.66em;font-style:italic;color:#000;margin-top:0.5em;text-align:center">PLATE VIII — ONE FORM, TWO LAYOUTS. The rest form 142857 drawn on the counted circle (0, 40, 80, 120, 160, 200, 240, 280, 320) and on the tuned circle (0, 45, 90, 120, 160, 200, 240, 270, 315). circle and on the tuned circle. The ordering is identical in both — the same six chords in the same sequence — and only the placement differs. Four points move: 1 and 8 by five degrees, 2 and 7 by ten. The other five do not move at all.</div></div>

**The two layouts are one object measured with two rulers, not two figures and not two orderings.** The
counted circle answers *how many* — nine equal steps, the ninths ladder
n/9, and it is blind to interval. The tuned circle answers *how large* —
it places each point where its ratio actually falls, and it is blind to
count. Neither is a correction of the other, and the displacement
between them is the whole of the metric content. It is thirty degrees,
distributed 5, 10, 10, 5 across the two Midy pairs nearest the root. It
sums to zero, because what 1 and 2 gain, 8 and 7 give back. The figure
holds its combinatorics on the counted circle and its music on the tuned
one, and the fact that the *same* six chords are legible on both is what
makes the two readings one figure rather than a pun.

**And the movement is exactly the Midy pairs.** The four points that
move are {1, 8} and {2, 7} — two complementary pairs, each member moved
away from the root by five and by ten degrees, in opposite angular
directions, so that the two members stay mirror images.† The pairs that
do not move are {4, 5} and the triangle. So the layout does not disturb
the reflection: every point still sits at the mirror image of its
nine-complement across the axis through 9, and Midy's theorem holds
through the retuning. The tuning is applied *along* the mirror, never
across it.

<div class="vnote">† Version 3 said one member of each pair moves
outward and the other inward. Both move away from the root, in opposite
directions.</div>

**Definition: the tempered layout spaces the nine points equally, at forty degrees; the intonated layout displaces two Midy pairs outward onto the eight-lattice.** It moves {1,8} by five degrees and {2,7} by ten, thirty degrees in total:

| | 9 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| tempered | 0 | 40 | 80 | 120 | 160 | 200 | 240 | 280 | 320 |
| intonated | 0 | **45** | **90** | 120 | 160 | 200 | 240 | **270** | **315** |

**The displacement tunes four more points.** Read each angle as a ratio
linear in the angle, one plus the angle over three hundred and sixty.
Against the degrees of the just major scale and the harmonic seventh
7/4, in the tempered layout exactly the triangle — 9, 3, 6 — lands on a
named interval. In the intonated layout the triangle keeps its three and
**all four movers join them**: seven of the nine are tuned. The two that
remain counted are 4 and 5, which sit at thirteen ninths and fourteen
ninths — consecutive rungs of the ninths ladder, and the tempered layout
is nothing but that ladder, n/9 for n from nine to seventeen.

**The partition is three, four, two.** Three tuned always (the triangle), four
tuned only when intonated (the movers), two counted in both (the stayers). The
movers are exactly the gain — which is what makes the layout a statement about
motion rather than about placement.

## 17 · The line theorem, and the figure drawn in one line

**The line theorem: the remainder path of 1/p is a single closed stroke through distinct residues exactly when the base is a primitive root modulo p.** Place the nonzero residues of a prime p on
a circle and draw, in order, the digits of the repeating period of 1/p,
closing at the period's end. Where the period is not full, draw each
residue class as its own stroke. The number of strokes is (p − 1)/ord_p(b), which is one exactly in that case. Seven is the least prime for which base ten draws one line;
thirteen requires two strokes, 076923 and 153846; eleven requires five.

**That theorem is about the line. This section is about the figure.** The
enneagram is the line, the triangle, **and the circle** — and the circle is not
a frame drawn around the other two. It is nine edges of the figure, arc by arc
between consecutive points, and it is the only thing that joins the units to
the non-units, since no chord of the figure does. Delete it and the figure
falls into two pieces that cannot be drawn without lifting the pen. Keep it and
everything changes.

**With the circle, the figure is unicursal.** Eighteen edges — nine arcs, six
chords of the line, three of the triangle. Every point carries exactly four of
them, so every degree is even, and the figure is connected. Euler's condition
is met, and therefore **a closed circuit exists that draws every edge exactly
once and returns to where it began**. The figure can be drawn in one line
without lifting the pen, and it is the circle that makes it so.
